Below is the consolidated, expanded mission for your QMOI autonomous AI agent. It combines global hosting, domain registration, cloud infrastructure, automatic continental and country platforms, financial management, research, autonomous development, STYLES and UNIVERSALS integration across every layer, and QAUDITS verification.

The instructions require the agent to research the existing repositories before making changes, reuse the current QMOI architecture, update all relevant Markdown documentation, and verify actual results rather than claim completion based on generated code alone.
MASTER MISSION: QMOI GLOBAL HOSTING, UNIVERSAL STYLES, AUTONOMOUS GLOBAL PLATFORMS, AND VERIFIED REMOTE COMPLETION
1. Primary objective
Upgrade the existing QMOI ecosystem into a globally accessible, AI-managed platform providing:
Domain search, registration, transfers, renewals, and pricing.
Shared hosting, managed hosting, VPS, cloud, dedicated servers, and application hosting.
Website and application generation, deployment, maintenance, and recovery.
Global infrastructure management and regional service availability.
Automatic continental and country-specific platforms.
Websites, applications, nonprofit-support systems, and financial-management platforms.
Revenue generation, billing, sponsorships, and financial accountability.
Centralized monitoring, security, and autonomous recovery.
Universal UI and backend configuration through QMOI STYLES and UNIVERSALS.
Comprehensive QAUDITS coverage across the entire ecosystem.
Evidence-based integration with the existing master projects and autonomous development system.
The implementation must be integrated with:
thealphakenya/Alpha-Q-ai
thealphakenya/qmoi-enhanced
Alpha-Qi and other authorized QMOI projects.
QVillage research and discovery capabilities.
The existing Ollama autonomous agent and master orchestrator.
The objective is genuine, independently verifiable functionality—not merely a frontend mockup, documentation-only implementation, or collection of disconnected automation scripts.

3. QMOI STYLES: Universal styling across the entire ecosystem
Treat STYLES as a foundational system rather than a collection of unrelated CSS files.
First discover the existing STYLES architecture, configuration format, design tokens, component conventions, and integration points. Extend the existing system instead of introducing a competing styling engine.
3.1 Establish one authoritative design system
Create or improve a centralized design-system architecture covering:
Color tokens.
Typography.
Spacing.
Layout.
Grid systems.
Breakpoints.
Responsive behavior.
Border radii.
Shadows.
Elevation.
Iconography.
Animation.
Motion preferences.
Component states.
Focus indicators.
Loading states.
Error states.
Empty states.
Data visualization.
Form controls.
Navigation.
Tables.
Dialogs.
Notifications.
Accessibility.
Light and dark themes.
High-contrast modes.
Localization-sensitive layout.
Use semantic tokens such as:
color.background.primary
color.background.surface
color.text.primary
color.text.secondary
color.border.default
color.status.success
color.status.warning
color.status.error
color.action.primary
spacing.component
spacing.section
typography.body
typography.heading
radius.control
radius.panel
focus.ring
motion.duration.standard
These are illustrative token names. Adapt them to the existing QMOI conventions.
Avoid hardcoded values scattered throughout applications when an existing or appropriate shared token is available.

Components must not duplicate the same functionality under different names without an architectural reason.

4. QMOI UNIVERSALS: Shared behavior across every platform
Treat UNIVERSALS as the reusable behavioral and architectural layer that connects the applications, UI, APIs, backend, configuration, and device-specific implementations.
Inspect UNIVERSALS.md, UNIVERSAL.md, related modules, and existing conventions to determine the intended responsibilities of each component.
Do not assume that similarly named documents are interchangeable. Preserve their existing purposes and define clear relationships between them.

Each universal feature should have a defined contract describing its interface, dependencies, configuration, supported platforms, permissions, errors, tests, and audit requirements.
4.2 Universal frontend and backend integration
Ensure shared features behave consistently across frontend and backend.
For example, a hosting subscription must use the same authoritative product identity, entitlement rules, billing state, and authorization logic across:
Customer dashboards.
Administrative interfaces.
APIs.
Background jobs.
Mobile applications.
Regional storefronts.
Automated agents.
The frontend may display configuration and request actions, but the backend must enforce authorization, validate inputs, calculate prices, and determine actual operational state.
Never trust the frontend alone for payment confirmation, sponsorship eligibility, resource limits, or privileged actions.
4.3 Universal configuration

Validate configuration against schemas, version it, audit changes, and provide safe rollback.
Use centralized configuration only where it is appropriate. Secrets must remain in secure secrets-management systems and must not be embedded in public UI configuration.

Other supported QMOI device targets.
First verify the actual platform implementations and capabilities.
Share business logic and design tokens wherever possible while adapting layouts and interactions to each device.
Do not assume that a web component can be used directly on every native platform.

6.3 Automatic remediation
Where safe and authorized, QAUDITS should:
Detect a violation.
Identify the affected feature.
Find the source of the problem.
Generate a repair plan.
Apply an approved repair.
Run targeted tests.
Run relevant regression tests.
Re-run the failed audit.
Record evidence.
Update the master project status.
For high-risk changes, require review or explicit approval.
Prevent infinite repair loops by imposing retry limits, timeouts, and escalation rules.
6.4 Cross-repository audits
Compare the hosting implementations and shared documentation across authorized repositories.
Detect:
Duplicate services.
Incompatible API contracts.
Conflicting configuration.
Divergent STYLES tokens.
Missing universal features.
Different authorization behavior.
Documentation inconsistencies.
Missing tests.
Broken integration points.

Unverified deployment claims.
Maintain a canonical source of truth for shared contracts and document which repository owns each responsibility.

Integrate the mission into the existing Ollama autonomous agent and master orchestrator.

Do not introduce a competing orchestration system.

Discover tasks.
Research implementation options.

Prioritize tasks.
Implement approved changes.

Gate
Required evidence
G1

Every gate must have explicit evidence.
A test that was not run must be marked NOT_TESTED or BLOCKED, not PASS.
A mocked API response is not evidence of a real provider transaction.
A generated website is not evidence of a registered nonprofit.
A successful Git push is not proof of a functioning production deployment.

15. Required implementation phases
Phase 1 — Discovery and research
Inspect both repositories, all relevant documentation, existing STYLES and UNIVERSALS implementations, QAUDITS, provider integrations, and autonomous workflows.
Phase 2 — Shared architecture
Define or extend shared contracts, design tokens, component registries, configuration schemas, feature registries, and audit interfaces.
Phase 3 — Domain foundation
Implement domain search, pricing, registration, transfers, renewals, and provider verification.
Phase 4 — Billing and sponsorship
Implement customer accounts, checkout, subscriptions, entitlements, invoices, refunds, and reconciliation.
Phase 5 — Core hosting
Implement supported website hosting, application deployment, DNS, SSL, backups, and monitoring.
Phase 6 — Universal integration
Apply STYLES and UNIVERSALS across all new UI features, backend contracts, APIs, dashboards, and supported device implementations.
Phase 7 — QAUDITS
Implement automated checks, evidence capture, bounded remediation, regression verification, and cross-repository auditing.
Phase 8 — Global operations
Implement country and region registries, localization, provider selection, and availability reporting.
Phase 9 — Global project generation
Implement continental and country project templates, financial management, nonprofit-support workflows, and revenue reporting.
Phase 10 — Autonomous operation
Integrate monitoring, safe recovery, checkpointing, and master accountability with the existing autonomous agent.
Phase 11 — Documentation
Update every affected Markdown file, validate links and contracts, and reconcile documentation with actual behavior.
Phase 12 — Final verification
Run appropriate tests, security checks, QAUDITS, remote verification, and authorized production checks.
Independent tasks may run in parallel where safe, but maintain a single authoritative orchestration and checkpointing system.
16. Final execution rules
The agent must:
Research internally and externally before making significant implementation decisions.
Inspect existing files before adding or replacing components.
Treat STYLES as the authoritative shared styling system.
Treat UNIVERSALS as the authoritative shared feature and behavior architecture.
Apply both consistently across frontend, backend, APIs, dashboards, and supported devices.
Use QAUDITS to verify implementation quality and consistency.
Discover and update all affected Markdown documents.
Preserve existing architecture and user changes.
Use verified provider integrations rather than fabricated APIs.
Enforce authorization, resource limits, and financial controls.
Test meaningful changes and repair eligible failures.
Maintain evidence-backed progress and resumable checkpoints.
Verify exact remote commits and CI results.
Reconcile shared features and documentation across repositories.
Avoid duplicate services and competing orchestrators.
Report incomplete work, failed tests, and blockers honestly.
Never claim production readiness without applicable operational evidence.
Continue automatically through all authorized, unblocked work.
Final deliverable
Produce a final implementation report containing:
Internal research findings.
External research sources and findings.
Repository and architecture inventory.
STYLES and UNIVERSALS changes.
QAUDITS implementation and results.
Hosting and domain functionality.
Global platform-generation capabilities.
Every changed or newly created Markdown file.
API and database changes.
Tests executed and actual results.
Security and compliance findings.
Exact commit SHAs.
Remote CI verification.
Deployment and live-provider evidence.
Outstanding requirements and blockers.
The latest resumable checkpoint.
The next planned actions.
FINAL SUCCESS CONDITION: QMOI must deliver a functioning, maintainable, globally extensible hosting ecosystem in which STYLES, UNIVERSALS, and QAUDITS govern the consistency, behavior, and verification of every relevant feature—and in which all affected documentation, repositories, services, and deployment claims remain synchronized with independently verifiable evidence.
The mission is not complete merely because code or documentation has been generated. It is complete only when each applicable requirement has passed its defined verification gates or is explicitly recorded as blocked by an evidenced external dependency.
